Homometric subsets of ℤ n \mathbb{Z}_{n} with cardinality 5: classification and enumeration
William Q. Erickson, Nicholas B. Jones

Abstract
. Two subsets of ℤ n \mathbb{Z}_{n} are said to be homometric if they have the same multiset of pairwise cyclic (i.e., Lee) distances. Homometric subsets necessarily have the same cardinality, say k k . In this paper, for all positive integers n n , we classify the homometric subsets of ℤ n \mathbb{Z}_{n} with cardinality k = 5 k=5 (modulo cyclic shifts and reflections). Our classification consists of six families of homometric pairs, and one family of homometric triples. We also give a closed-form generating function that counts these homometric pairs and triples for all n n . The same problem for k ≤ 4 k\leq 4 was partially solved by Erdős and ultimately settled by Rosenblatt–Berman (1984). As an immediate application of our result, one obtains an explicit criterion for the solvability of the crystallographic phase retrieval problem, in the setting of binary signals supported on five atoms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 94A12; Secondary 05A15; 00A65

1.
Introduction

The crystallographic binary phase retrieval problem

X-ray crystallography is the main technique for determining the atomic structure of molecules.
In order to investigate the intrinsic structure of a crystalline substance, an X-ray beam is directed at the substance, which causes the beam to diffract.
The
phase retrieval problem
is to recover the molecular structure of the substance from its diffraction pattern.
This problem admits many different variations.
In one such variant, the unknown structure of a periodic crystal (along one of the lattice vectors defining a unit cell) is given by some subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{n}=\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
In this setting, all atoms in the crystal are identical, and
S
can be viewed as a binary signal (i.e., an indicator function) on
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
The data obtained from the corresponding diffraction pattern are the magnitudes of the values (not the values themselves) of the discrete Fourier transform
\hat{S}
; this explains the term “phase retrieval,” since the phase information of the signal is lost in passing from
\hat{S}
to
|\hat{S}|
.
Thus, in this setting, the essence of the phase retrieval problem is to recover
S
from
|\hat{S}|
.

Non-retrievability and homometric bracelets

In the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the recovery of
S
from
|\hat{S}|
is only ever possible up to cyclic shifts and reflections (i.e., modulo the action of the dihedral group
D_{2n}
on the usual circular depiction of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
); indeed, if
S
is obtained from
T
by a shift and/or reflection, then automatically
|\hat{S}|=|\hat{T}|
.
For this reason, it is most natural to work with the combinatorial objects known as
binary bracelets
of length
n
, which can be viewed as subsets of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
(where the black beads represent the elements of a given subset), such that all cyclic shifts and reflections are considered the same bracelet.
We write
[S]
to denote the bracelet that has
S
as one of its representatives (i.e.,
[S]
is the
D_{2n}
-orbit of
S
); the
weight
of
[S]
is the number of black beads, that is, the cardinality of
S
.
A bracelet
[S]
is said to be
non-retrievable
if it cannot be recovered from
|\hat{S}|
.
This raises the key question in crystallographic phase retrieval, and in the present paper:
which bracelets
[S]
are non-retrievable?

Non-retrievability can be understood more concretely by introducing the notion of homometry.
It is well known
[
6
]
*p. 1489 that
|\hat{S}|=|\hat{T}|
if and only if the bracelets
[S]
and
[T]
are
homometric
, that is, they have the same multiset of pairwise distances between their black beads.
For example, shown below are two distinct homometric bracelets of length
n=12
and weight
5
, along with their multisets of distances:

(1.1)

\text{Distances: }\{\mskip-5.0mu\{1,1,2,2,3,3,4,5,5,6\}\mskip-5.0mu\}&&\text{Distances: }\{\mskip-5.0mu\{1,1,2,2,3,3,4,5,5,6\}\mskip-5.0mu\}\end{array}

(These are typically called
Patterson diagrams
, after
[
21
]
.)
It follows that a bracelet is non-retrievable if and only if it is homometric with a different bracelet; thus the bracelets shown in (
1.1
) are both non-retrievable.
In this way, classifying non-retrievable bracelets is the same as describing the equivalence classes of bracelets with respect to the homometry relation — in particular, characterizing those equivalence classes that contain
more
than one bracelet.
Following the phase retrieval literature, we call these
nontrivial homometry classes
.
Note that if
[S]
and
[T]
are homometric, then
S
and
T
necessarily have the same cardinality; thus we may as well restrict our attention to those binary bracelets with some fixed weight
k
.
We therefore have the following combinatorial restatement of the crystallographic phase retrieval problem:

Problem 1.1
.

Let
k
be a positive integer.
For all positive integers
n
, classify and enumerate the nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets with length
n
and weight
k
.

Problem
1.1
has so far been solved only for
k\leq 4
.
In fact, for
k\leq 3
, the problem is trivial, since then every homometry class is trivial (i.e., every binary bracelet is retrievable).
For
k=4
, a solution to Problem
1.1
was (according to
[
25
]
*p. 337) partially discovered by Erdős, and was ultimately proved by Rosenblatt–Berman
[
25
]
*Thm. 3.9.
This classification of nontrivial homometry classes for
k=4
consists of two types, which we depict below by giving the locations of the four black beads :

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\right\}\\[25.83325pt]
\text{$n$ divisible by $4$},&&\text{$n$ divisible by $13$}\\
1\leq i<n/4&&\end{array}

For
k\geq 5
, however, the homometry classes are not nearly as well behaved, leading Rosenblatt to observe that the
k\geq 5
case seems very difficult to classify
[
25
]
*p. 329.

In this paper we solve Problem
1.1
for
k=5
.
Our solution consists of a classification theorem (Theorem
4.1
) and an enumeration theorem (Theorem
4.4
), which we preview below.

Theorem

(Classification theorem; see also Theorem
4.1
)
.

Consider the set of binary bracelets of length
n
and weight 5.
Every nontrivial homometry class belongs to exactly one of the seven types
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
in Figure
1
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right\}

Type
\mathsf{G}

n
divisible by
20

Figure 1.
Main result of the paper: a classification of nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets of length
n
and weight
5
.
We draw hash marks dividing each circle into equal arcs.
A dot drawn on a hash mark represents a black bead whose position is fixed; all other dots represent black beads whose position varies with the parameters
i
or
j
.
Note that one must exclude
i
- and
j
-values that cause two black beads to coincide.
(See Theorem
4.1
for the bracelets written out in explicit coordinates, along with the complete description of valid
i
- and
j
-values.)
If a pair of beads is attached to the centerpoint by dashed lines, then the two beads maintain a constant distance as
i
varies.
In Types
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{E}
this distance is
n/2
; in Type
\mathsf{B}
it is
2n/5
(left bracelet) and
n/5
(right bracelet); in Type
\mathsf{C}
it is
n/6
; in Type
\mathsf{D}
it is
n/3
; and in Type
\mathsf{F}
it is
n/4
.
The vertical dashed line in Type
\mathsf{A}
(resp.,
\mathsf{D}
) remains vertical as
j
(resp.,
i
) varies.

For example, the homometric pair in (
1.1
) is of Type
\mathsf{A}
, where
n=12
with
(i,j)=(1,2)
.
As expected, our classification confirms that the homometry classes for
k=5
are much more complicated than for
k=4
.
In particular, there is a
two
-parameter family of homometric pairs (Type
\mathsf{A}
), as well as a family of homometric
triples
(Type
\mathsf{E}
).
Despite the complicated structure of these nontrivial homometry classes, our theorem above leads us to the following closed-form generating function to count these classes for arbitrary
n
.

Theorem

(Enumeration theorem; see also Theorem
4.4
)
.

Let
h_{n}
denote the number of nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets with length
n
and weight 5.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}\,x^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2x^{10}}{(1-x^{2})(1-x^{4})^{2}}+\frac{x^{10}+4x^{15}}{(1-x^{5})(1-x^{10})}+\frac{x^{12}+x^{18}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})}+\frac{4x^{16}}{(1-x^{8})^{2}}+\frac{4x^{20}}{1-x^{20}}

\displaystyle=3x^{10}+3x^{12}+6x^{14}+5x^{15}+10x^{16}+14x^{18}+22x^{20}+20x^{22}+31x^{24}+10x^{25}+30x^{26}+\cdots.

By means of this generating function, one can compute any
h_{n}
by taking the coefficient of
x^{n}
in the Maclaurin expansion.
For example, it takes an ordinary desktop computer just a few seconds to compute
h_{15000}=14068747
.
In fact, by making a trivial adjustment (
4.9
) to the generating function, one computes that of these
14068747
nontrivial homometry classes,
1249
are triples and the rest are pairs.

The special case
k=5
is notable for the following reason.
In general, one can replace
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
by
\mathbb{Z}
in the above discussion, replacing circular distance by the standard absolute value metric.
There certainly do exist mutually homometric subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
(modulo translation and reflection);
moreover, two homometric subsets
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
descend to homometric length-
n
bracelets for any
n
, via the obvious map
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n}
given by
x\mapsto x\>{\rm mod}\>n
.
The opposite is
not
true, however; indeed,
\mathbb{Z}
(unlike
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
) has no mutually homometric subsets with at most 5 elements (modulo translation and reflection); see
[
15
]
*p. 9.
Thus,
5
is the largest value of
k
for which the crystallographic binary phase retrieval problem depends entirely on the periodicity of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
In other words, for
k\geq 6
, some of the nontrivial homometry classes in
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
are merely “shadows” of those in
\mathbb{Z}
.

Overview of the paper

In order to obtain our two main theorems (previewed above) in Section
4
, we transfer the problem from the discrete setting (for the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
) to a more straightforward continuous setting (for the circle group
\mathbb{T}
).
Thus, Sections
2
and
3
focus on the analogous problem of homometric 5-element subsets of
\mathbb{T}
.
Throughout those sections, we study
5-point bracelets
, that is, orbits of 5-point subsets of
\mathbb{T}
under rotations and reflections.
In order to control the enormous number of possible configurations of two 5-point bracelets, we first divide these configurations into five geometric categories labeled by ordered pairs
(p,q)
.
Then we introduce a bookkeeping device we call a
(p,q)
-difference table
(Definition
2.7
), along with an algorithm to obtain the minimal set of 22 tables that exhaust all possible homometric configurations.
For each of these difference tables there is a corresponding
solution set
(Definition
2.8
) consisting of ordered pairs of vectors that encode pairs of homometric 5-point bracelets.
By (very carefully) reparametrizing these 22 solution sets, we are able to stitch them together into a seven-type classification (see Proposition
3.2
).
Then in Section 4, we translate this classification back into the discrete setting for
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, finally obtaining Theorems
4.1
and
4.4
.
We conclude in Section
5
with some further questions and open problems (some of which have since been solved by P. Methawisal
[
18
]
).

The collision-free version and Piccard’s “theorem”

A closely related question in the literature was the existence of homometric sets which are
collision-free
, meaning that their common multiset of distances contains no repeated distances.
Originally, an apparent result of Sophie Piccard
[
23
]
asserted that distinct homometric subsets of the real line (up to translation and reflection) cannot be collision-free;
however, this “theorem” turned out to be false, as it admits precisely two families of counterexamples
[
7
,
2
,
27
]
.
(In both families, the collision-free homometric sets have cardinality
k=6
.)
In light of this historical interest, a referee suggested that we use our computer code to observe the possible numbers of
collisions
, that is, the number of distances that repeat, in each of the seven families in our classification theorem above:

Type

Number of collisions

\mathsf{A}

0, 1, 2, 3, or 4

\mathsf{B}

3 or 5

\mathsf{C}

1 or 4

\mathsf{D}

0 or 2

\mathsf{E}

1

\mathsf{F}

0 or 2

\mathsf{G}

1

In particular, for the modular version of the
k=5
problem addressed in this paper, there are infinite families of collision-free homometric pairs occurring in Types
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{D}
, and
\mathsf{F}
.

Crystallography background

Since its origins in X-ray crystallography in the early 1900s, the importance of the phase retrieval problem has increased due to many additional modern applications; the related literature is therefore vast, and the references we mention here form the merest skeleton of a survey.
We first recommend the excellent exposition by Bendory–Edidin
[
6
]
, in particular pages 1489–1490 explaining the periodic binary setting that is the subject of the present paper.
The references in that expository article include many very recent papers, reflecting the ongoing interest in crystallographic phase retrieval; see
[
5
,
3
,
4
,
19
]
, for example.

As mentioned above, the classification problem we address was proposed in 1984 by Rosenblatt
[
25
]
, to which we refer the reader especially for the exposition in its first three sections.
In that paper and elsewhere
[
24
]
are some beautiful results giving algebraic characterizations of non-retrievable signals; at the same time, however, “algebraic techniques are typically not very
useful in designing and analyzing practical and efficient algorithms”
[
6
]
*p. 1494 for phase retrieval.
(See, however, the elegant proofs in
[
13
]
, where homometric triples are classified using Rosenblatt’s notion of
spectral units
.)
We are thus hopeful that the concrete classification we give in this paper might ultimately be aligned with, and perhaps even shed some light upon, the elegant algebraic theory.

For readers interested in the pioneering work in crystallographic phase retrieval, we point out Pauling–Shappell
[
22
]
in 1930, as well as the seminal 1944 paper by Patterson
[
21
]
, which (at least implicitly) contained the two-type classification for
k=4
later proved by Rosenblatt.
In the 1960s, Bullough
[
8
]
investigated various general properties of homometric subsets of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
Papers such as
[
15
,
14
]
and references therein treat the equivalent problem (called the “beltway” or “turnpike” problem, depending on whether the setting is periodic or aperiodic, respectively) of reconstructing discrete sets from the multiset of their interpoint distances.
Erdős took an avid interest in this and several related problems
[
9
]
.

The Z-relation in microtonal music theory

The phase retrieval problem in the setting of this paper is equivalent to a well-known problem in microtonal music theory.
In the language of music theory, one views
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
as a scale dividing the octave into
n
equally-tempered pitches; the special case
n=12
, shown above in (
1.1
), corresponds to the standard 12-pitch scale typical in Western music.
A subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is a
chord
(or “pitch class set”);
in our special case, a subset
S
with cardinality
k=5
is typically called a
pentachord
.
The cyclic distance between two pitches is called the
interval
between them, and the multiset of cyclic distances is called the
interval content
of a chord.
Given two chords that are not obtained from one another via
transposition
and/or
inversion
— that is, chords corresponding to distinct bracelets — the two chords are said to be
Z-related
if their multisets of pairwise intervals coincide — that is, if they are homometric.
Therefore, the main result of this paper can be restated as follows, using the language of music theory: we classify and enumerate the Z-related pentachords in an equal-temperament scale consisting of an arbitrary number of pitches.

To the reader interested in the musical motivation, we recommend the work of Forte
[
10
]
(who coined the term “Z-related”), Soderberg
[
26
]
, and more recent work such as
[
16
,
17
]
.
In particular, we strongly recommend the monumental dissertation
[
11
]
(and the references therein), which gives a comprehensive historical and musical overview of the Z-relation along with an original mathematical treatment.
The notion of homometry has also been adapted to rhythms rather than chords
[
20
]
.
Pertaining to Problem
1.1
in the present paper, we point out the note
[
12
]
, which confirmed that the classification problem was very much open for
k\geq 5
and included some enumerative computer data in various special cases.
Closely related to our paper are the notes by Althuis–Göbel
[
1
]
, which contain a few special families of Z-related pentachords.
In particular, their Properties 3 and 4 are very special cases of our Type
\mathsf{A}
, while their Property 6 is a special case of our Type
\mathsf{B}
.
After completing the present paper, we became aware of the very recent result of Jedrzejewski
[
13
]
*Thm. 6, classifying all triples of Z-related pentachords; this coincides with our Type
\mathsf{E}
.

2.
Homometry and difference tables for the circle group

In this and the next section, in order to temporarily bypass the bracelet length
n
, we conduct our analysis not in terms of the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, but rather the circle group
\mathbb{T}\cong\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

5-point bracelets and their canonical vectors

The notion of homometry, described in the introduction, applies equally well to finite subsets of the circle group
\mathbb{T}
, up to equivalence under rotations and reflections.
To refer concisely to such an object — that is, the continuous analogue of a binary bracelet with weight
k
— we introduce the term
k
-point bracelet
as follows.

Definition 2.1

(
k
-point bracelet)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}\coloneqq[0,1)
be the circle group, where the operation is addition modulo
\mathbb{Z}
.
By depicting
\mathbb{T}
as a circle (identifying 0 and 1), we obtain a natural action on
\mathbb{T}
by the orthogonal group
\operatorname{O}(2)
consisting of all rotations and reflections of the circle.
By a
k
-point bracelet
, we mean the
\operatorname{O}(2)
-orbit
[S]
of some
k
-element subset
S\subset\mathbb{T}
.

NB:
In writing down an arbitrary representative
[s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}]
of a
k
-point bracelet, we view the
s_{i}
’s modulo
\mathbb{Z}
;
hence the
s_{i}
’s may lie outside the interval
[0,1)
.

Let
S
and
T
be finite subsets of
\mathbb{T}
.
It follows from Definition
2.1
that
[S]=[T]
if and only if there is some
a\in\mathbb{T}
such that

(2.1)

T=\{a+s:s\in S\}\text{ or }T=\{a-s:s\in S\}.

In both cases in (
2.1
),
T
is obtained from
S
via rotating by
a
, and in the second case this rotation is composed with the reflection
x\mapsto-x=1-x
.

The cyclic (i.e., Lee) distance on
\mathbb{T}
is given by

(2.2)

d(t,u)=d_{\mathbb{T}}(t,u)\coloneqq\min\{|t-u|,\>1-|t-u|\}\in[0,\>1/2].

For this reason, we say that an element
t\in\mathbb{T}
is
short
if
t\leq 1/2
, and
long
if
t>1/2
.
The distinction is key to our analysis because for all
t,u\in\mathbb{T}
with
t\geq u
, we have

(2.3)

d(t,u)=\begin{cases}t-u,&\text{$t-u$ is short},\\
1-(t-u),&\text{$t-u$ is long}.\end{cases}

Two
k
-point bracelets are said to be
homometric
if they have the same multiset of pairwise distances between their elements (upon choosing any representative for each bracelet).
Homometry is clearly an equivalence relation on the set of all
k
-point bracelets.

Definition 2.2

(Homometry class)
.

A
homometry class
is an equivalence class of
k
-point bracelets with respect to the homometry relation.
A homometry class is
nontrivial
if it has cardinality greater than 1.

From now on, we specialize to
k=5
.
We begin our analysis by classifying 5-point bracelets into four types as follows.
If one draws any representative of a 5-point bracelet on a circle, and then draws all 10 chords connecting its 5 points, then this creates a circumscribed pentagon whose interior is divided into 11 regions: five
outer triangles
(sharing a side with the pentagon), five
inner triangles
(sharing a vertex but not a side with the pentagon), and one small
central pentagon
(disjoint with the large pentagon).
In order to make these 11 regions mutually disjoint, we set the following boundary convention: an outer triangle does
not
contain its boundary along its outer side; an inner triangle does
not
contain its boundary along its two outer sides; and the central pentagon does
not
contain its boundary at all.
(See the three shaded regions, respectively, in Figure
2
.)
With these conventions, every point in the interior of the large pentagon lies in exactly one of these 11 regions.

Definition 2.3

(Long count)
.

Consider a 5-point bracelet, depicted with the ten pairwise chords between its points as described above.
The
long count
of the 5-point bracelet is defined to be

\begin{cases}0,&\text{center of the circle lies outside the interior of the large pentagon},\\
1,&\text{center of the circle lies in an outer triangle},\\
2,&\text{center of the circle lies in an inner triangle},\\
3,&\text{center of the circle lies in the central pentagon}.\end{cases}

Long count 0
Long count 1
Long count 2
Long count 3

Figure 2.
Examples accompanying Definition
2.3
, and the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
For long count 0, 1, or 2, this is the unique representative constructed in the proof of Lemma
2.5
.

See Figure
2
for examples.
(Alternatively, one could define the long count to be the minimal number of chords intersecting a line drawn from the center of the circle to the exterior of the pentagon.)
For the motivation behind the term “long count”, see (
2.14
) below.

Lemma 2.4
.

If two distinct homometric 5-point bracelets have long counts
p
and
q
(assuming
p\leq q
), then
(p,q)\in\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(1,2),(2,2)\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(p,q)=(0,0)
.
Then in each bracelet, every pairwise difference is short, and therefore by (
2.3
) every pairwise
distance
is just the pairwise
difference
.
But it is well known
[
15
]
*p. 8 that 5 points on the real line are determined (up to translation and reflection) by their multiset of pairwise differences, and therefore the bracelets in question are not distinct, which is a contradiction.
Thus
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
.

Next suppose that
p\leq 2
and
q=3
.
Consider a generic bracelet
[Y]
with long count 3, as pictured on the far right-hand side of Figure
2
.
In particular, note the five
outer distances
(i.e., the arc lengths between two consecutive points) and the remaining five
inner distances
.
Since each inner distance is at most 1/2, it is the sum of the two consecutive outer distances between its endpoints.
Therefore
[Y]
satisfies the following:

(2.4)

The sum of the outer distances

\displaystyle=1,

(2.5)

the sum of the inner distances

\displaystyle=2.

Thus all ten distances in
[Y]
sum to 3, and we note that in fact each distance is
strictly
less than
1/2
.
By homometry, the same must be true for the bracelet
[X]
with long count
p
.
In particular, the center of the circle for
[X]
must lie outside the boundary of the central pentagon.
But then in
[X]
, there is at least one inner distance that is strictly less than the sum of the two consecutive outer distances between its endpoints.
Therefore, as opposed to
[Y]
, the ten distances in
[X]
sum to strictly less than 3.
It follows that
[X]
and
[Y]
have distinct distance multisets, and therefore are not homometric, which is a contradiction.
Thus
(p,q)\notin\{(0,3),(1,3),(2,3)\}
.

Finally, suppose that
(p,q)=(3,3)
.
Then there exist distinct homometric bracelets
[X]
and
[Y]
, both with long count 3.
For both
[X]
and
[Y]
, the following geometric facts are immediately apparent from the picture of a bracelet with long count 3 (far right-hand side of Figure
2
), in addition to the facts (
2.4
)–(
2.5
) above:

(2.6)

The sum of two consecutive outer distances

\displaystyle<1/2,

(2.7)

the sum of two consecutive outer distances

\displaystyle>\text{the nonadjacent outer distance},

(2.8)

the sum of three consecutive outer distances

\displaystyle>1/2,

where on the right-hand side of (
2.7
), the “nonadjacent outer distance” means the unique remaining outer distance which is not adjacent to either of the two consecutive outer distances on the left-hand side of (
2.7
).
In the bracelet
[X]
, denote consecutive outer distances by
a,b,c,d,e
, which determines the inner distances
a+b,\ldots,e+a
.
We first observe that if the outer distances in
[Y]
were to include three inner distances of
[X]
, then we would contradict (
2.4
), because by (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) any three inner distances in
[X]
sum to
>1
.
Therefore the outer distances of
[Y]
include at most two inner distances of
[X]
.
We now treat each case (0, 1, and 2) separately.

Case 0: The outer distances of
[Y]
include 0 inner distances of
[X]
.

In this case, the arrangement of the outer distances in
[Y]
is obtained by permuting that in
[X]
.
Modulo rotations and reflections, there are only three nontrivial permutations of the five outer distances
a,\ldots,e
:

•

Adjacent transposition
(12)
.

Suppose the outer distances of
[Y]
are ordered
bacde
.
Then the locations in
[Y]
of the inner distances
a+b
and
c+d
and
d+e
are forced.
This leaves two ways to assign the distances
b+c
and
a+e
:
either
a+c=b+c
(so that
a=b
) or
a+c=a+e
(so that
c=e
).
In either case, we have equality of bracelets
[X]=[Y]
, which is a contradiction.

•

Cycle
(123)
.

Suppose the outer distances of
[Y]
are ordered
cabde
.
Then the locations in
[Y]
of the inner distances
a+b
and
d+e
are forced.
There are three ways to assign the distance
b+c
.
If
a+c=b+c
, then
a=b
, so that the outer distances of
[Y]
are ordered
cbade=abced
, which is an adjacent transposition (see previous item).
Likewise, if
b+d=b+c
, then
c=d
, giving the ordering
dabce=abced
, which is again an adjacent transposition.
Finally, if
c+e=b+c
, then
b=e
, giving the ordering
caedb=acbde
, which is an adjacent transposition.
Therefore, by the previous item, we have
[X]=[Y]
, a contradiction.

•

Cycle
(1342)
.

Suppose the outer distances of
[Y]
are ordered
bdace
.
Then one of the inner distances must equal
b+d
.
If this distance is
a+b
or
b+c
or
c+d
or
d+e
, then the resulting equality of outer distances implies that the outer distance ordering is actually an adjacent 3-cycle (see previous item).
Thus we must have
a+e=b+d
.
Proceeding in this way to assign all inner distances, we likewise obtain
a+d=b+c
and
a+c=d+e
and
a+b=c+e
, which forces
a=b=c=d
.
Therefore we have
[X]=[Y]
, a contradiction.

Case 1: The outer distances of
[Y]
include 1 inner distance of
[X]
.

In this case, the outer distances of
[Y]
consist of four outer distances of
[X]
, along with one inner distance of
[X]
.
But applying (
2.4
) to
[Y]
, we see that this inner distance of
[X]
must equal the omitted outer distance of
[X]
.
Thus we are actually in Case 0, so we are done.

Case 2: The outer distances of
[Y]
include 2 inner distances of
[X]
.

Let
\mathbf{i}=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
denote the pair of inner distances of
[X]
that are outer distances of
[Y]
, and let
\mathbf{o}=\{o_{1},o_{2}\}
denote the pair of outer distances of
[X]
that are
not
outer distances of
[Y]
.
By applying (
2.4
) to both
[X]
and
[Y]
, we have

(2.9)

i_{1}+i_{2}=o_{1}+o_{2}.

Note that the chords representing two inner distances either intersect each other in the interior of the circle, or else share an endpoint on the circle itself.
Thus, up to relabeling by rotations and reflections, there are two possibilities for
\mathbf{i}
, where (without loss of generality) we take
i_{1}=a+b
:

•

Suppose
\mathbf{i}=\{a+b,\>b+c\}
.
If
\mathbf{o}=\{a,b\}
or
\{a,c\}
or
\{b,c\}
, then
i_{1}+i_{2}>o_{1}+o_{2}
, violating (
2.9
).
If
\mathbf{o}=\{b,d\}
, then we have
a+b+c=d
, which violates (
2.6
) and (
2.8
).
Likewise, if
\mathbf{o}=\{d,e\}
, then we have
a+2b+c=d+e
, which violates (
2.6
) and (
2.8
).
If
\mathbf{o}=\{c,d\}
, then we have
a+2b=d
, which violates (
2.7
), since
a
and
b
are consecutive outer distances, and
d
is the outer distance nonadjacent to them.
This leaves only the case where
\mathbf{o}=\{c,e\}
, meaning that the set of outer distances of
[Y]
is
\{a,b,d,a+b,b+c\}
; but by (
2.6
) and (
2.8
),
b+c
cannot be adjacent to
a
or
a+b
or
d
, so this case is impossible.

•

Suppose
\mathbf{i}=\{a+b,\>c+d\}
.
Note that
\mathbf{o}
must contain
e
;
otherwise, we would have
i_{1}+i_{2}=a+b+c+d>o_{1}+o_{2}
, contradicting (
2.9
).
If
\mathbf{o}=\{a,e\}
, then we have
b+c+d=e
, which violates (
2.6
) and (
2.8
).
This leaves only the case
\mathbf{o}=\{b,e\}
, meaning that the set of outer distances of
[Y]
is
\{a,c,d,a+b,c+d\}
.
To avoid violating (
2.6
), the order of the outer distances of
[Y]
(up to cyclic shifts and reflections) is given either by
a,c,c+d,d,a+b
, or (via interchanging
a
and
d
) by
d,c,a+c,a,b+d
.

First suppose the order of the outer distances of
[Y]
is
a,c,c+d,d,a+b
.
We must have
b=a+c
, since putting
b
as the sum of any other two consecutive outer distances would force
a+b>1/2
, violating (
2.6
).
Next, applying (
2.4
) to both
[X]
and
[Y]
, we obtain the equation
a+b+c+d+e=2a+b+2c+2d
, which yields
e=a+c+d=b+d
; adding
a
to both sides, we have
a+e=(a+b)+d
.
One of the three remaining distances (i.e.,
e
,
b+c
, and
d+e
) must equal the sum
d+(c+d)
.
Since
d+e=1-(a+b+c)=(2a+b+2c+2d)-(a+b+c)=2d+a+c>2d+c
, the inner distance in question cannot be
d+e
.
Likewise, if we were to have
b+c=d+(c+d)
, then we would have
b=2d
; but starting from (
2.4
) applied to
[Y]
, this would give
1=2a+b+2c+2d=2a+2b+2c
, so that
a+b+c=1/2
, violating (
2.8
).
Therefore we must have
e=d+(c+d)
.
This leaves two ways to assign the remaining inner distances
b+c
and
d+e
.
For each assignment, we solve for
(a,b,c,d,e)
subject to the constraints (
2.4
)–(
2.8
):

–

Putting
b+c=c+(c+d)
and
d+e=a+(a+b)
yields

\textstyle(a,b,c,d,e)=\left(a,\frac{1}{3}(1-2a),\frac{1}{3}(1-5a),a,\frac{1}{3}(1+a)\right),\quad\text{for }\frac{1}{14}<a<\frac{1}{8}.

Comparing this to the outer distances of
[Y]
given in order

\textstyle a,\>\frac{1}{3}(1-5a),\>\frac{1}{3}(1-2a),\>a,\>\frac{1}{3}(1+a),

we would have
[X]=[Y]
, a contradiction.

–

Putting
b+c=a+(a+b)
and
d+e=c+(c+d)
yields

\textstyle(a,b,c,d,e)=\left(\frac{1}{11},\frac{3}{11},\frac{2}{11},\frac{1}{11},\frac{4}{11}\right).

Comparing this to the outer distances of
[Y]
given in order

\textstyle\frac{1}{11},\frac{2}{11},\frac{3}{11},\frac{1}{11},\frac{4}{11},

we would have
[X]=[Y]
, a contradiction.

Since each of these two solutions above has
a=d
, there is no need to check the remaining ordering of outer distances of
[Y]
.

Having shown that all three cases (0, 1, and 2) lead to a contradiction, we conclude that
(p,q)\neq(3,3)
.
∎

In light of Lemma
2.4
, we may restrict our attention to 5-point bracelets with long count 0, 1, or 2.
We will identify each such bracelet with a unique 4-dimensional vector; by working directly with these
canonical vectors
(Definition
2.6
), we will avoid any redundancy involving various representatives of the same bracelet.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
p\in\{0,1,2\}
.
A 5-point bracelet has long count
p
if and only if it has a representative
\{0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
satisfying the following inequalities:

(2.10)

\displaystyle x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{4},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{4}\leq 1/2,&p=0,\\
x_{4}>1/2\textup{ and }x_{3}\leq 1/2\textup{ and }x_{4}-x_{1}\leq 1/2,&p=1,\\
x_{3}>1/2\textup{ and }x_{2}<1/2\textup{ and }x_{4}-x_{1}\leq 1/2,&p=2,\end{cases}

(2.12)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{1}\leq x_{4}-x_{3},\textup{ and if }x_{1}=x_{4}-x_{3}\textup{ then }x_{2}-x_{1}\leq x_{3}-x_{2},&p=0\textup{ or }1,\\
x_{1}\leq 1-x_{4},\;\;\textup{ and if }x_{1}=1-x_{4}\;\>\textup{ then }x_{2}-x_{1}\leq x_{4}-x_{3},&p=2.\end{cases}

Moreover, this representative is unique.

Proof.

Consider a 5-point bracelet with long count
p
.
This bracelet has several representatives containing the element
0
; any such representative can thus be written in the form
\{0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
with the
x_{i}
’s ordered according to (
2.10
).
We must show that there is exactly one such representative satisfying (
2.11
)–(
2.12
).
The proof follows almost immediately from the geometry of the various long counts
p
, so we will work with the depiction of
\mathbb{T}
as a circle with
0
on top and with values increasing clockwise.
(See Figure
2
.)

Suppose
p=0
.
Then by Definition
2.3
, the center of the circle lies outside the interior of the pentagon formed by
0
and the
x_{i}
’s.
Therefore there are at most two ways to place the
x_{i}
’s such that they are all on the right-hand half of the circle (including the bottom point 1/2), thereby satisfying (
2.11
).
(See the
p=0
example in Figure
2
.)
In particular, these two ways are reflections of each other, via the reflection that interchanges
0
and
x_{4}
; the condition (
2.12
), specifying the relative edge lengths of the pentagon, is true for exactly one of these reflections.

Suppose
p=1
.
Then the center of the circle lies in an outer triangle.
Therefore there are at most two ways (again via the reflection
0\leftrightarrow x_{4}
) to place the
x_{i}
’s such that this triangle has outer vertices at
0
and
x_{4}
.
(See the
p=1
example in Figure
2
.)
In either such placement, the center of the circle must lie strictly to the right of the chord between
0
and
x_{4}
, and weakly to the left of the chord between
0
and
x_{3}
, and weakly above the chord between
x_{1}
and
x_{4}
; thus (
2.11
) is satisfied.
The edge length condition (
2.12
) is true for exactly one of the two reflections.

Suppose
p=2
.
Then the center of the circle lies in an inner triangle.
There are at most two ways (via reflecting the circle horizontally) to place the
x_{i}
’s such that 0 is the top vertex of this inner triangle.
(See the
p=2
example in Figure
2
.)
In either such placement, the center of the circle must lie strictly to the left of the chord between
0
and
x_{3}
, strictly to the left of the chord between
0
and
x_{2}
, and weakly above the chord between
x_{1}
and
x_{4}
; thus (
2.11
) is satisfied.
The edge condition (
2.12
) is true for exactly one of the two horizontal reflections.

The converse follows immediately from the geometric argument above.
∎

Definition 2.6

(Canonical vector of a bracelet)
.

Given a 5-point bracelet with long count
p\in\{0,1,2\}
, its
canonical vector
is the vector
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
whose coordinates are given by the unique representative specified in Lemma
2.5
.

Difference tables

Because our immediate goal is to characterize homometric pairs
\{[X],[Y]\}
, we will treat
[X]
as an unknown, meaning that the coordinates
x_{i}
of its canonical vector
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
should be treated as variables.
We set the following shorthand for the pairwise differences between the
x_{i}
’s (where
x_{0}\coloneqq 0
):

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq x_{j}-x_{i},

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{ij}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\boxed{\mathsf{ij}},\text{ so that }\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{\overline{ij}}}}=\boxed{\mathsf{ij}},

(2.13)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}:0\leq i<j\leq 4\right\}=\left\{\boxed{\mathsf{01}},\boxed{\mathsf{02}},\boxed{\mathsf{03}},\boxed{\mathsf{04}},\boxed{\mathsf{12}},\boxed{\mathsf{13}},\boxed{\mathsf{14}},\boxed{\mathsf{23}},\boxed{\mathsf{24}},\boxed{\mathsf{34}}\right\}.

The term “long count” is now justified by the fact that the long count of
[X]
equals the number of long elements of
D
.
We say that an element of
D
is
p
-long
if that element is long when
[X]
has long count
p
.
Concretely, by (
2.11
), we have the following:

(2.14)

\text{The $p$-long elements of $D$ are }\begin{cases}\text{none},&p=0,\\
\boxed{\mathsf{04}},&p=1,\\
\text{$\boxed{\mathsf{03}}$ and $\boxed{\mathsf{04}}$},&p=2.\end{cases}

Consider the following staircase arrangement of the elements of
D
:

(2.15)

\mathsf{01}

\mathsf{04}

\mathsf{14}

24

\mathsf{23}

\mathsf{34}

Note that the entries in the top row of (
2.15
) recover the canonical vector
\mathbf{x}
, since
\boxed{\mathsf{0j}}=x_{j}
.
Moreover, since
x_{j}-x_{i}=(x_{i+1}-x_{i})+(x_{i+2}-x_{i+1})+\cdots+(x_{j}-x_{j-1})
, each off-diagonal entry is the sum of the diagonal entries lying weakly southwest of it:

(2.16)

\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}=\sum_{d=i}^{j-1}\boxed{\mathsf{d,d+1}}\qquad\text{for all six pairs $(i,j)$ such that $j-i>1$}.

Definition 2.7

(Difference tables)
.

Let
(p,q)\in\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(1,2),(2,2)\}
.
Each permutation
\pi
of the set
D
gives rise to a unique
(p,q)
-difference table
, obtained from (
2.15
) as follows:

•

Permute the entries in (
2.15
) via
\pi
.

•

Place a bar over each
p
-long entry.

•

Place a bar over the entry in each position corresponding to a
q
-long entry in (
2.15
).

We define

\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)\coloneqq\Big\{\text{$(p,q)$-difference tables}\Big\},

which contains
10!
elements (one for each permutation
\pi
of
D
).
Given a difference table
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
, we write

\displaystyle\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{ij})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\text{ the entry of $\Delta$ in the position corresponding to $\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}$ in~\eqref{table X}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\>\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{01})},\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{02})},\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{03})},\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{04})}\>\right)=\text{top row of $\Delta$}.

The idea is to view each
(p,q)
-difference table
\Delta
as a generic pair of homometric bracelets with long counts
p
and
q
.
In particular, the bracelet with long count
p
is assumed to have canonical vector
\mathbf{x}
, and the bracelet with long count
q
is assumed to have canonical vector
\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
.
In light of the geometric constraints on canonical vectors (see Lemma
2.5
), the ultimate goal will be to characterize all pairs
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(\Delta))\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
which truly give canonical vectors of 5-point bracelets.
This motivates the following definition.
(See Appendix
A
for a full example in which we construct a difference table and implement the following definition of its
solution set
.)

Definition 2.8

(Solution set of a difference table)
.

Fix
(p,q)\in\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(1,2),(2,2)\}
, and consider the following linear system of equations and inequalities in the variable
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
:

(2.17)

\displaystyle 0<x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{4},

(2.18)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\boxed{\mathsf{04}}\leq 1/2,&p=0,\\
\boxed{\mathsf{04}}>1/2\text{ and }\boxed{\mathsf{03}}\leq 1/2\text{ and }\boxed{\mathsf{14}}\leq 1/2,&p=1,\\
\boxed{\mathsf{03}}>1/2\text{ and }\boxed{\mathsf{02}}<1/2\text{ and }\boxed{\mathsf{14}}\leq 1/2,&p=2,\end{cases}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\boxed{\mathsf{01}}\leq\boxed{\mathsf{34}},\text{ and if }\boxed{\mathsf{01}}=\boxed{\mathsf{34}}\text{ then }\boxed{\mathsf{12}}\leq\boxed{\mathsf{23}},&p=0\text{ or }1,\\
\boxed{\mathsf{01}}\leq 1-\boxed{\mathsf{04}},\text{ and if }\boxed{\mathsf{01}}=1-\boxed{\mathsf{04}}\text{ then }\boxed{\mathsf{12}}\leq\boxed{\mathsf{34}},&p=2,\end{cases}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{ij})}=\sum_{d=i}^{j-1}\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{d,d+1})}\quad\text{for all six pairs $(i,j)$ such that $j-i>1$},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\text{same as~\eqref{ineqs long count p} and~\eqref{ineqs canonical ordering p}, but with each $\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}$ replaced by $\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{ij})}$ and with $p$ replaced by $q$},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}(\Delta),&p\neq q,\\
\mathbf{x}<\mathbf{y}\text{ in lexicographical order,}&p=q,\end{cases}

where the lexicographical condition means that the first nonzero coordinate of
\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}
is negative.
(The purpose of this condition is to prevent both
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
from occurring in
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
.)
For
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
, define

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big\{\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:\text{$\mathbf{x}$ satisfies the system \eqref{positive}--\eqref{noncongruent}}\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big\{\big(\mathbf{x},\>\mathbf{y}(\Delta)\big):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)\Big\}.

We call
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
the
(p,q)
-solution set
of
\Delta
(or just the
solution set
, when the
(p,q)
is clear from context).

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(p,q)\in\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(1,2),(2,2)\}
.
We have a bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{unordered pairs of distinct}\\
\textup{homometric 5-point bracelets}\\
\textup{with long counts $p$ and $q$}\end{array}\right\}\longrightarrow\;\;\bigcup_{\mathclap{\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)}}\>\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta),

given by mapping an unordered pair of bracelets to the unique ordered pair of its canonical vectors occurring in the right-hand side.

Proof.

Let
[X]
and
[Y]
be distinct homometric 5-point bracelets, with canonical vectors
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
, respectively.
Suppose
[X]
has long count
p
, and
[Y]
has long count
q
; if
p=q
, assume (without loss of generality) that
\mathbf{x}<\mathbf{y}
with respect to lexicographical order.
We must first show that there exists some
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
such that
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
.

By definition of homometry,
[X]
and
[Y]
have the same distance multiset;
thus there is a permutation
\pi
of
D
such that, for all
\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}\in D
,

d(y_{i},y_{j})=d(x_{i^{\prime}},x_{j^{\prime}})\text{ if }\pi\!\left(\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}\right)=\boxed{\mathsf{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}},

where we set
x_{0}=y_{0}\coloneqq 0
.
Let
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
be the
(p,q)
-difference table corresponding to this permutation
\pi
.
To show that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)
, we verify each constraint (
2.17
)–(
2.22
).
The constraints (
2.17
)–(
2.19
) are automatically satisfied due to (
2.10
)–(
2.12
).
Next, consider the construction of
\Delta
according to Definition
2.7
, after having permuted the entries of (
2.15
) via
\pi
.
In particular, by first placing a bar over the
p
-long entries, we render all entries short (since
[X]
has long count
p
);
consequently, the position corresponding to each
\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}
in (
2.15
) contains the entry
d(y_{i},y_{j})=d(x_{i^{\prime}},x_{j^{\prime}})
rather than the entry
x_{j^{\prime}}-x_{i^{\prime}}
.
By then placing a bar in each position corresponding to a
q
-long entry in (
2.15
), we guarantee that each

(2.23)

\boxed{\Delta(\mathsf{ij})}=y_{j}-y_{i}

rather than
d(y_{i},y_{j})
.
Therefore the relations (
2.16
) carry over to their corresponding positions in
\Delta
, and thus (
2.20
) is satisfied.
In particular, (
2.23
) implies that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
, and therefore (
2.21
) is satisfied due to (
2.11
)–(
2.12
).
By this fact
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
, our initial assumptions on
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
also imply (
2.22
).
Having thus established that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)
, and that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
, it follows by Definition
2.8
that
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
.
Moreover, by (
2.22
) it is impossible that
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
occurs in
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
for some
(p,q)
-difference table
\Delta^{\prime}
, and so the map in the lemma is well-defined.

To show that the map is a bijection, we exhibit its inverse as follows.
Let
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
, and let
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
, with coordinates given by
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
.
By Definition
2.8
, we have
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
.
Set
X=\{0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
and
Y=\{0,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}\}
.
By Lemma
2.5
and by (
2.17
)–(
2.19
),
[X]
has canonical vector
\mathbf{x}
and long count
p
.
By (
2.20
), we have
0<y_{1}<y_{2}<y_{3}<y_{4}<1
.
The relations (
2.20
) also imply (
2.23
) for every entry of
\Delta
; thus (using the same argument as in the first part of the proof describing the bars in Definition
2.7
) by (
2.21
) and the fact that
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
,
[Y]
has canonical vector
\mathbf{y}
and long count
q
.
Reversing the argument in the first part of the proof, we have an equality of multisets
\{\mskip-5.0mu\{d(x_{i},x_{j}):0\leq i<j\leq 4\}\mskip-5.0mu\}=\{\mskip-5.0mu\{d(y_{i},y_{j}):0\leq i<j\leq 4\}\mskip-5.0mu\}
, and thus
[X]
and
[Y]
are homometric.
Finally, (
2.22
) guarantees that
[X]\neq[Y]
.
Hence the unordered pair
\{[X],[Y]\}
is the preimage of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.
∎

3.
Classification of homometry classes for the circle group

The main result of this section (Proposition
3.2
) is a classification of the nontrivial homometry classes of 5-point bracelets.
In light of Lemma
2.9
, for each
(p,q)
, we seek a concrete description of the union

(3.1)

\bigcup_{\mathclap{\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)}}\>\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta),

where
\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
is the set of
(p,q)
-difference tables (Definition
2.7
) and
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
is the solution set of
\Delta
(Definition
2.8
).
Indeed, (
3.1
) is a sort of “state space” (via canonical vectors) for all unordered distinct homometric pairs with long counts
p
and
q
.
We emphasize the word “pairs” in the previous sentence, because we do require more than a description of (
3.1
) in order to detect full homometry
classes
, rather than just homometric
pairs
.
For example, distinct homometric
triples
will correspond to ordered triples
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(\Delta),\mathbf{y}(\Delta^{\prime}))
with distinct entries, where
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)\cap\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
for difference tables
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
and
\Delta^{\prime}\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q^{\prime})
.
For this reason, we truly do need to work with the difference tables themselves, rather than simply passing directly to the union (
3.1
).

This, of course, raises the difficulty of working with the unwieldy number (
10!\approx 3.6\times 10^{6}
) of difference tables in each
\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
.
One should suspect, however, that many of the solution sets
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
are in fact empty, or (more generally) are properly contained in other solution sets
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
.
It turns out that this is true of nearly
all
difference tables.
To make this rigorous, we implement the following simple algorithm, which outputs a minimal set
\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)\subset\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)
such that

(3.2)

\bigcup_{\mathclap{\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(p,q)}}\>\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)=\>\>\bigcup_{\mathclap{\Delta\in\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)}}\>\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta).

Note that the system (
2.17
)–(
2.22
), which defines each solution set
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
, consists entirely of linear equations and inequalities, and therefore is quite straightforward to program.

Algorithm 3.1
.

Input:
a pair
(p,q)\in\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(1,2),(2,2)\}
.

Output:
a minimal set
\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)
satisfying (
3.2
).

•

Initialize
A\leftarrow\varnothing
.

•

For each permutation
\pi
of
D
(the set defined in (
2.13
)):

–

Construct the
(p,q)
-difference table
\Delta
corresponding to
\pi
, as described in Definition
2.7
.

–

If there exists some
\Delta^{\prime}\in A
such that
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)\subseteq\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
, then skip this
\pi
and continue to the next permutation.

–

Else, update
A\leftarrow\Big(A\setminus\{\Delta^{\prime\prime}\in A:\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)\}\Big)\cup\{\Delta\}
.

•

Set
\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)\coloneqq A
.

Implementing Algorithm
3.1
for each
(p,q)
returns the output shown below.
1
1

1

Our Mathematica notebook can be accessed at
https://github.com/WilliamQErickson/homometry-proof
.

In particular, out of a total of
5\times 10!\approx
18 million difference tables, we can restrict our attention to the following 22:

\displaystyle\rsfscr{M}\!(0,1)

0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\mathsf{01}$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 1380.4pt}}$}}&\\[17.22217pt]
\mathsf{B_{4}}&\mathsf{B_{5}}&\mathsf{F_{4}}\end{array}\right\}.

Our labeling scheme above will ultimately align with the seven types
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
in our main classification result; Type
\mathsf{E}
is missing at this stage because it consists of homometric triples, which will require further analysis below in (
3.3
).
Although the order of taking permutations in the algorithm affects which particular tables constitute the output, nonetheless these 22 tables
are
uniquely determined up to equivalence, where
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
are considered equivalent if
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)=\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
.

In Appendix
B
, we record the explicit solution set
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
for each of the 22 tables
\Delta
shown above; these are easily obtained by computer using Definition
2.8
, and are parametrized in terms of certain free variable(s)
x_{i}
.
We have placed them in Appendix
B
because we will still display them all — after carefully reparametrizing them — throughout the proof of Proposition
3.2
below (giving each explicit reparametrization so that it can be easily lined up with the data in Appendix
B
, if the reader so desires).
It turns out that these 22 solution sets are
almost
disjoint; more precisely,
\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)\cap\mathbf{X}_{pq^{\prime}}(\Delta^{\prime})
is empty for
almost
all distinct
\Delta\in\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)
and
\Delta^{\prime}\in\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q^{\prime})
.
In fact, checking all such pairs by computer, we obtain the following three nonempty intersections:

(3.3)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|l|}\hline\cr(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})&(p,q,q^{\prime})&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\Big\{\big(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(\Delta),\mathbf{y}(\Delta^{\prime})\big):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)\cap\mathbf{X}_{pq^{\prime}}(\Delta^{\prime})\Big\}}}\\
\hline\cr(\mathsf{A_{1}},\mathsf{A_{2}})&(0,1,1)&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left\{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
1/6+x_{1}\\
1/3+x_{1}\\
1/2\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
1/6\\
1/3\\
1/2+x_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/6-x_{1}\\
1/6\\
1/3\\
2/3-x_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right):0<x_{1}\leq 1/12\right\}}}\\
\hline\cr(\mathsf{A_{3}},\mathsf{B_{3}})&(1,1,1)&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left\{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/10\\
3/10\\
4/10\\
6/10\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2/10\\
3/10\\
4/10\\
7/10\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2/10\\
3/10\\
4/10\\
7/10\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)\right\}}}\\
\hline\cr(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D_{4}})&(1,2,2)&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left\{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}3/12\\
4/12\\
5/12\\
8/12\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}4/12\\
5/12\\
7/12\\
8/12\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}4/12\\
5/12\\
7/12\\
8/12\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)\right\}}}\\
\hline\cr\text{All other pairs}&-&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\varnothing}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Upon inspecting the vectors in (
3.3
), it is clear that every intersection
\mathbf{X}_{pq}(\Delta)\cap\mathbf{X}_{pq^{\prime}}(\Delta^{\prime})\cap\mathbf{X}_{pq^{\prime\prime}}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})
for
three
distinct difference tables is empty.

Proposition 3.2
.

Every nontrivial homometry class of 5-point bracelets belongs to exactly one of the following seven types:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type $\mathsf{A}$:}&\Big\{\left[0,t,t+u,\frac{1}{2}+t-u,\frac{1}{2}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{1}{2}+t,t+u,\frac{1}{2}+t-u,\frac{1}{2}\right]\Big\},\\[4.30554pt]
&\text{for all $t,u\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\right)$ such that $t\neq u$, and if $u=\frac{1}{6}$ then $t=\frac{1}{12}$.}\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{B}$:}&\Big\{\left[0,t,\frac{1}{5},\frac{2}{5},\frac{2}{5}+t\right],\;\left[0,t,\frac{1}{5},\frac{1}{5}+t,\frac{3}{5}\right]\Big\},\text{ for all }t\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right]\setminus\left\{\frac{1}{10},\frac{1}{5},\frac{3}{10},\frac{2}{5}\right\}.\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{C}$:}&\Big\{\left[0,\frac{1}{6}+t,\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3}+t,\frac{2}{3}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3}+t,\frac{1}{2}+t,\frac{2}{3}\right]\Big\},\text{ for all }t\in\left(0,\frac{1}{12}\right].\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{D}$:}&\Big\{\left[t,\frac{1}{6}-t,\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3}+t,\frac{1}{2}\right],\;\left[0,t,\frac{1}{6}-t,\frac{1}{3}+t,\frac{5}{6}\right]\Big\},\text{ for all }t\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\right)\setminus\left\{\frac{1}{12},\frac{1}{6}\right\}.\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{E}$:}&\Big\{\left[0,t,\frac{1}{6}+t,\frac{1}{3}+t,\frac{1}{2}\right],\;\left[0,t,\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2}+t\right],\;\left[0,\frac{1}{6}-t,\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}-t\right]\Big\},\text{ for all }t\in\left(0,\frac{1}{12}\right).\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{F}$:}&\Big\{\left[0,t,\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{4}+t,\frac{1}{2}\right],\;\left[0,t,\frac{1}{8}+t,\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}+t\right]\Big\},\text{ for all }t\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)\setminus\left\{\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{8}\right\}.\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{G}$:}&\phantom{\text{or }}\big\{\left[0,\frac{1}{20},\frac{2}{20},\frac{6}{20},\frac{9}{20}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{3}{20},\frac{4}{20},\frac{5}{20},\frac{11}{20}\right]\big\}\\[4.30554pt]
&\text{or }\big\{\left[0,\frac{1}{20},\frac{4}{20},\frac{7}{20},\frac{9}{20}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{3}{20},\frac{4}{20},\frac{6}{20},\frac{11}{20}\right]\big\}\\[4.30554pt]
&\text{or }\big\{\left[0,\frac{4}{20},\frac{5}{20},\frac{7}{20},\frac{13}{20}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{7}{20},\frac{8}{20},\frac{11}{20},\frac{13}{20}\right]\big\}\\[4.30554pt]
&\text{or }\big\{\left[0,\frac{2}{20},\frac{7}{20},\frac{8}{20},\frac{11}{20}\right],\;\left[0,\frac{5}{20},\frac{9}{20},\frac{11}{20},\frac{12}{20}\right]\big\}.\end{array}

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4
and by (
3.2
), there is a bijection

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigcup_{(p,q)}\left(\bigcup_{\Delta\in\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)}\hskip-8.61108pt\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)\right)&\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textup{unordered pairs of distinct}\\
\textup{homometric 5-point bracelets}\end{array}\right\},\\
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})&\longmapsto\big\{[0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}],\>[0,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}]\big\}.\end{split}

Thus, in order to describe the codomain in (
3.4
), we can simply inspect the 22 solution sets
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
, viewing their elements
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
as pairs of homometric bracelets via (
3.4
).
As mentioned above, the solution sets
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
are given in Appendix
B
; here, however, we will approach them sequentially by Type
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
(rather than by long count
(p,q)
as in Appendix
B
), in order to line them up via (
3.4
) with the classification in this proposition.
Crucially, for each
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
we rewrite the free variable(s)
x_{i}
in terms of a new parameter
t
(and
u
, in Type
\mathsf{A}
), which effectively “stitches together” the various solution sets so as to coincide with the parametrization given in the proposition.
We must also remember to account for the intersections (
3.3
) when we encounter the difference tables
\mathsf{A_{1}}
,
\mathsf{A_{2}}
,
\mathsf{A_{3}}
,
\mathsf{B_{3}}
,
\mathsf{C}
, and
\mathsf{D_{4}}
, so as to avoid overlaps between types.

Throughout the proof, given
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
for some
\Delta
, we will write
X\coloneqq\{0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
and
Y\coloneqq\{0,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}\}
to denote the representatives on the right-hand side of (
3.4
).

Type
\mathsf{A}
.

There are three difference tables we labeled as Type
\mathsf{A}
:

\Delta

(p,q)

Parameters

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)

For all
t,u
:

\mathsf{A_{1}}

(0,1)

\begin{aligned} t&=x_{1},\\
u&=x_{2}-x_{1}\end{aligned}

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}t\\
t+u\\
1/2+t-u\\
1/2\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}t\\
u\\
1/2-u\\
1/2+t\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)}}

\begin{array}[]{c}0<t<u/2<1/8\\
\text{or}\\
0<t=u/2\leq 1/12\\
\end{array}

\mathsf{A_{2}}

(0,1)

\begin{aligned} t&=1/2-x_{3},\\
u&=x_{3}-x_{2}\end{aligned}

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}u-t\\
1/2-t-u\\
1/2-t\\
1/2\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}t\\
u\\
1/2-u\\
1/2+t\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)}}

\begin{array}[]{c}0<u/2<t<u<1/4\\
\text{or}\\
1/12\leq t=u/2<1/8\end{array}

\mathsf{A_{3}}

(1,1)

\begin{aligned} t&=1/2+x_{1}-x_{3},\\
u&=1/2-x_{3}\end{aligned}

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}t-u\\
1/2-2u\\
1/2-u\\
1/2+t-u\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/2-t-u\\
1/2-2u\\
1/2-u\\
1-t-u\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)}}

0<u<t<1/4

For each
\Delta
above, we will use the
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
column to check that
\{[X],[Y]\}
is the same unordered pair of bracelets given in the proposition for Type
\mathsf{A}
.
In light of (
2.1
), we reproduce those two bracelet representatives in the top row of the following table, then express them as rotations and/or reflections of
X
and
Y
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|r|r|}\hline\cr&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left[0,\;t,\;t+u,\;\frac{1}{2}+t-u,\;\frac{1}{2}\right]}}&\left[0,\;\frac{1}{2}+t,\;t+u,\;\frac{1}{2}+t-u,\;\frac{1}{2}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{A_{1}}}}&[X]&\left[\left\{\left(\frac{1}{2}+t\right)-y:y\in Y\right\}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{A_{2}}}}&\left[\left\{\frac{1}{2}-x:x\in X\right\}\right]&\left[\left\{\left(\frac{1}{2}+t\right)-y:y\in Y\right\}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{A_{3}}}}&\left[\left\{\left(\frac{1}{2}+t-u\right)-x:x\in X\right\}\right]&\left[\left\{\left(t+u\right)+y:y\in Y\right\}\right]\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

It remains to verify the values of the parameters
t
and
u
given in the proposition, namely,
t,u\in\left(0,1/4\right)
such that
t\neq u
, and if
u=1/6
then
t=1/12
.
Combining
\mathsf{A_{1}}
,
\mathsf{A_{2}}
, and
\mathsf{A_{3}}
in the first table above, we confirm that
t,u\in(0,1/4)
with
t\neq u
.
By the table of intersections (
3.3
),
\mathbf{X}_{0,1}(\mathsf{A_{1}})
and
\mathbf{X}_{0,1}(\mathsf{A_{2}})
intersect precisely when
u=1/6
for both
\mathsf{A_{1}}
and
\mathsf{A_{2}}
, and when the
t
for
\mathsf{A_{1}}
equals
1/6
minus the
t
for
\mathsf{A_{2}}
.
(This common value must equal the parameter
x_{1}
in (
3.3
).)
In this case, from the top row of (
3.3
), we see that
\mathbf{y}(\mathsf{A_{1}})\neq\mathbf{y}(\mathsf{A_{2}})
as long as
t\neq 1/12
(for either
\mathsf{A_{1}}
or
\mathsf{A_{2}}
; equivalently, for both of them).
Therefore, if
u=1/6
and
t\neq 1/12
, then the corresponding bracelets for
\mathsf{A_{1}}
and
\mathsf{A_{2}}
form a homometry class with
three
bracelets, which is therefore
not
of Type
\mathsf{A}
, but rather of Type
\mathsf{E}
.
We conclude that if
u=1/6
in Type
\mathsf{A}
, then necessarily
t=1/12
, as claimed.
In this special case
(t,u)=(1/12,1/6)
, we have
\mathbf{y}(\mathsf{A_{1}})=\mathbf{y}(\mathsf{A_{2}})=(1/12,1/6,1/3,7/12)
, so the corresponding homometry class (of two bracelets) is common to both
\mathsf{A_{1}}
and
\mathsf{A_{2}}
; this is no difficulty since our classification does not descend to the subtypes within Type
\mathsf{A}
.
Finally, we observe from (
3.3
) that there is a single point of intersection between
\mathbf{XY}\!_{1,1}(\mathsf{A_{3}})
and
\mathbf{XY}\!_{1,1}(\mathsf{B_{3}})
; we choose to keep this point
(t,u)=(1/5,1/10)
in Type
\mathsf{A}
,
so we will remember to delete it from Type
\mathsf{B}
.

Type
\mathsf{B}
.

There are six difference tables we labeled as Type
\mathsf{B}
:

\Delta

(p,q)

Parameter

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)

For all
t
:

\mathsf{B_{2}}

(1,1)

\mathsf{B_{3}}

(1,1)

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2/5-t\\

\mathsf{B_{4}}

(2,2)

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/5\\

3/5\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}3/5-t\\
4/5-t\\

\mathsf{B_{5}}

(2,2)

t=3/5-x_{1}

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}3/5-t\\

3/5\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/5\\
t\\
3/5\\

\mathsf{B_{6}}

(1,1)

–

\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/10\\
3/10\\
5/10\\
6/10\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}3/10\\
4/10\\
5/10\\
8/10\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)}}

–

Using the
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
column, we verify for each
\Delta
that
[X]
and
[Y]
are the two bracelets given in the proposition (reproduced in the top row of the following table):

\begin{array}[]{|c|r|r|}\hline\cr&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left[0,\;t,\;\frac{1}{5},\;\frac{2}{5},\;\frac{2}{5}+t\right]}}&\left[0,\;t,\;\frac{1}{5},\;\frac{1}{5}+t,\;\frac{3}{5}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{B_{1}}}}&[X]&\left[\left\{\frac{3}{5}-y:y\in Y\right\}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{B_{2}}}}&[Y]&[X]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{B_{3}}}}&[Y]&\left[\left\{\frac{3}{5}-x:x\in X\right\}\right]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{B_{4}}}}&\left[\left\{\left(\frac{2}{5}+t\right)+y:y\in Y\right\}\right]&[X]\\
\hline\cr\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\mathsf{B_{5}}}}&\left[\left\{\left(\frac{2}{5}+t\right)+x:x\in X\right\}\right]&[Y]\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{B_{6}}&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\begin{array}[]{r}\text{Upon setting $t=\frac{1}{2}$:}\\[4.30554pt]
\left[\left\{\frac{1}{2}-x:x\in X\right\}\right]\end{array}}}&\begin{array}[]{r}\text{Upon setting $t=\frac{1}{2}$:}\\[4.30554pt]
\left[\left\{1-y:y\in Y\right\}\right]\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Having included

to account for

, we see that the union of the

.

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/10\\

was the point of intersection between

and

given in (

).
Since we retained this point in Type
\mathsf{A}
above, we delete it here from Type
\mathsf{B}

Remark 3.3
.

The four pairs in Type

(3.5)

\textstyle\left\{\left[0,\;\frac{5}{20},\;\frac{8t}{20},\;\frac{15+4t}{20},\;\frac{5-4t}{20}\right],\;\left[0,\;\frac{5}{20},\;\frac{4t}{20},\;\frac{15+8t}{20},\;\frac{15-4t}{20}\right]\right\},\quad\text{for $t=1,2,3,4$}.

In particular, with

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|r|r|}\hline\cr&t&\underset{\phantom{a}}{\overset{\phantom{a}}{\left[0,\;\frac{5}{20},\;\frac{8t}{20},\;\frac{15+4t}{20},\;\frac{5-4t}{20}\right]}}&\left[0,\;\frac{5}{20},\;\frac{4t}{20},\;\frac{15+8t}{20},\;\frac{15-4t}{20}\right]\\
