4.
Homometry classes of binary bracelets with weight
k=5

Having classified the nontrivial homometry classes of 5-point bracelets in Proposition
3.2
, we will now translate the result from the continuous setting (
\mathbb{T}
) into the discrete setting (
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
).
This section contains the main results of the paper — a classification theorem and an enumeration theorem — which together constitute a solution to Problem
1.1
in the case
k=5
.
In place of the 5-point bracelets from the previous sections, we now turn to binary bracelets with 5 black beads, in the typical sense of the combinatorics literature.

Restatement of the problem for
\mathbb{Z}_{n}

We briefly formalize the nontechnical description of Problem
1.1
given in the introduction.
The relevant definitions are completely analogous to those given in the continuous setting of Section
2
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, and let
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\coloneqq\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
denote the cyclic group under addition modulo
n
.
There is a natural action on
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
by the dihedral group
D_{2n}
, realized by arranging the elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
in order at the vertices of a regular
n
-gon.
For our purposes, we define a
binary bracelet of length
n
to be the
D_{2n}
-orbit of some subset
S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
The cardinality
k
is called the
weight
of the bracelet.
We depict this bracelet, denoted by
[S]=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}]
, as a circular arrangement of
n
beads, where the
k
black beads correspond to the elements of
S
.
As in Definition
2.1
, we note that when writing down an arbitrary representative
[s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}]
of a bracelet, we view the
s_{i}
’s modulo
n
, and so they themselves may lie outside
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
.

The cyclic (i.e., Lee) distance between two elements
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is given by

(4.1)

d(a,b)=d_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}(a,b)\coloneqq\min\{|a-b|,\>n-|a-b|\}.

Since the action of
D_{2n}
preserves distances, it makes sense to define the
distance multiset
of a binary bracelet to be the multiset of
\binom{k}{2}
pairwise distances between the black beads of the bracelet.
Two binary bracelets of the same length are said to be
homometric
if they have the same distance multiset.
Recall the diagram (
1.1
) for an example of two homometric bracelets
[0,6,7,9,11]
and
[0,5,8,10,11]
of length
n=12
and weight
k=5
.
The homometry relation is clearly an equivalence relation on the set of all binary bracelets of a given length
n
.
Thus it makes sense to adopt Definition
2.2
in this discrete setting: a
homometry class
is an equivalence class with respect to the homometry relation, defined on the set of all binary bracelets of a given length.
As before, we say that a homometry class is
nontrivial
if it has cardinality greater than 1.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, and consider the set of all binary bracelets with length
n
and weight 5.
Every nontrivial homometry class belongs to exactly one of the following seven types
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
, where
m
is a positive integer, and
i
and
j
are positive integers ranging over the index set
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\;\;)
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type $\mathsf{A}$ (for $n=2m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,i,i+j,m+i-j,m\right],\;\left[0,m+i,i+j,m+i-j,m\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}({\mathsf{A}})=\left\{(i,j):1\leq i,j<\tfrac{m}{2}\text{ and }i\neq j,\text{ and if }j=\tfrac{m}{3}\text{ then }i=\tfrac{m}{6}\right\}.\\[12.91663pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{B}$ (for $n=5m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,i,m,2m,2m+i\right],\;\left[0,i,m,m+i,3m\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}({\mathsf{B}})=\left\{i:1\leq i\leq\frac{5m}{2}\right\}\setminus\left\{\frac{m}{2},m,\frac{3m}{2},2m\right\}.\\[12.91663pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{C}$ (for $n=6m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,m+i,2m,2m+i,4m\right],\;\left[0,2m,2m+i,3m+i,4m\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{C})=\left\{i:1\leq i\leq\frac{m}{2}\right\}.\\[12.91663pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{D}$ (for $n=6m$):}&\Big\{\left[i,m-i,2m,2m+i,3m\right],\;\left[0,i,m-i,2m+i,5m\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{D})=\left\{i:1\leq i<\frac{3m}{2}\right\}\setminus\left\{\frac{m}{2},m\right\}.\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{E}$ (for $n=6m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,i,m+i,2m+i,3m\right],\;\left[0,i,m,2m,3m+i\right],\;\left[0,m-i,m,2m,4m-i\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{E})=\left\{i:1\leq i<\frac{m}{2}\right\}.\\[12.91663pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{F}$ (for $n=8m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,i,m,2m+i,4m\right],\;\left[0,i,m+i,2m,4m+i\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{F})=\left\{i:1\leq i<4m\right\}\setminus\left\{m,2m,3m\right\}.\\[12.91663pt]
\text{Type $\mathsf{G}$ (for $n=20m$):}&\Big\{\left[0,5m,8mi,15m+4mi,5m-4mi\right],\;\left[0,5m,4mi,15m+8mi,15m-4mi\right]\Big\},\\[8.61108pt]
&\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{G})=\left\{i:1\leq i\leq 4\right\}.\end{array}

Remark 4.2
.

Earlier in Figure
1
we depicted the diagrams corresponding to each type in the classification.
The parameters
i
and
j
in the diagrams coincide with those given in the theorem above;
in each diagram, we draw dashes dividing the circle into equal arcs, with each arc representing
m
beads in a bracelet.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Consider the map

(4.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{5-point bracelets}\\
\textup{with canonical vector $\mathbf{x}$}\\
\textup{such that $n\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}$}\end{array}\right\}&\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{binary bracelets}\\
\textup{with length $n$}\\
\textup{and weight $5$}\end{array}\right\},\\[4.30554pt]
[0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]&\longmapsto[0,nx_{1},nx_{2},nx_{3},nx_{4}].\end{split}

The map is well-defined, since both the
{\rm O}(2)
-action on
\mathbb{T}
and the
D_{2n}
-action on
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
are given by rotations and reflections, which commute with the scalar multiplication by
n
.
In fact, (
4.2
) is a bijection, since its inverse is given by taking any representative of a binary bracelet and dividing its elements by
n
.
Comparing (
2.2
) and (
4.1
), we observe that the distance
d_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
is obtained from the distance
d_{\mathbb{T}}
via multiplication by
n
;
consequently, the bijection (
4.2
) preserves the homometry relation.
Therefore, (
4.2
) descends to a bijection between the homometry classes in its domain and codomain.

Thus in order to write down the nontrivial homometry classes in the codomain of (
4.2
), we simply need to determine those in Proposition
3.2
whose canonical vectors
\mathbf{x}
are such that
n\mathbf{x}
has integer coordinates.
This is especially straightforward because in each homometry class given in Proposition
3.2
, there is a 5-point bracelet of the form
[0,z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},z_{5}]
given by a representative containing 0.
Therefore, its canonical vector
\mathbf{x}
is such that
n\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
if and only if each
nz_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since all
z_{i}
’s are rational, this occurs if and only if

•

n=\ell m
for some positive integer
m
, where
\ell
is the least common denominator of the
z_{i}
’s (upon setting
t=u=0
); and

•

nt
and
nu
are integers.

By inspecting the 5-point bracelet representatives in Proposition
3.2
, we immediately determine the least common denominator
\ell
as follows:

(4.3)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{Type}&\mathsf{A}&\mathsf{B}&\mathsf{C}&\mathsf{D}&\mathsf{E}&\mathsf{F}&\mathsf{G}\\
\hline\cr\ell&2&5&6&6&6&8&20\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Thus in order to translate from Proposition
3.2
to Theorem
4.1
, we take each 5-point bracelet representative, multiply its elements by
n
, and then express them in terms of the positive integers
m
,
i
, and
j
using the substitutions

m=\frac{n}{\ell},\qquad i=nt,\qquad j=nu.

The index sets
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\;\;)
are obtained in the same way from the
t
- and
u
-values given in Proposition
3.2
.
Note that for Type
\mathsf{G}
, we use the discrete parameter
t\in\{1,2,3,4\}
given in (
3.5
).
∎

We turn to the second part of Problem
1.1
, namely that of enumeration.
The goal is to understand the sequence
(h_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of nonnegative integers, where

h_{n}\coloneqq\#\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets}\\
\text{with length $n$ and weight 5}\end{array}\right\}.

A fundamental goal in enumerative combinatorics, when studying an unknown sequence, is to find a closed form for its generating function.
In particular, the
generating function
of the sequence
(h_{n})
is the formal power series

H(x)\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}\,x^{n}

whose coefficients give the terms of the sequence.
If we can find a closed form (such as a rational expression) for
H(x)
, then we automatically know the entire sequence
(h_{n})
via expanding the Maclaurin series of
H(x)
, ignoring any issues of convergence.

In the following lemma, we determine the generating functions for the sequences obtained by restricting the count
h_{n}
to each of the seven types in our classification.
This amounts to describing the cardinalities of the index sets
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\;\;)
in Theorem
4.1
.
In the proof, we will make use of the following well-known closed forms:

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y}{(1-y)},\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor m/2\rfloor y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{2}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}m\,y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y}{(1-y)^{2}},

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(m-1)y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{2}}{(1-y)^{2}},

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor^{2}\,y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{3}(1+y^{2})}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})^{2}}.

Lemma 4.3
.

For each type
\bullet\in\{\mathsf{A},\ldots,\mathsf{G}\}
in Theorem
4.1
, let

\displaystyle h_{n}(\bullet)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\#\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets}\\
\textup{with length $n$ and weight 5, of Type $\bullet$}\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle H(\bullet;x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(\bullet)\,x^{n}.

We have

\begin{array}[]{ll}H(\mathsf{A};x)=\dfrac{2x^{10}}{(1-x^{2})(1-x^{4})^{2}}-\dfrac{3x^{18}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})},&H(\mathsf{E};x)=\dfrac{x^{18}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})},\\[17.22217pt]
H(\mathsf{B};x)=\dfrac{x^{10}+4x^{15}}{(1-x^{5})(1-x^{10})},&H(\mathsf{F};x)=\dfrac{4x^{16}}{(1-x^{8})^{2}},\\[17.22217pt]
H(\mathsf{C};x)=\dfrac{x^{12}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})},&H(\mathsf{G};x)=\dfrac{4x^{20}}{1-x^{20}}.\\[17.22217pt]
H(\mathsf{D};x)=\dfrac{3x^{18}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})},&\end{array}

Proof.

Let
\ell
be the least common denominator given in (
4.3
) for Type
\bullet
.
By Theorem
4.1
, we have
h_{\ell m}(\bullet)=|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\bullet)|
for every positive integer
m
, and we have
h_{n}(\bullet)=0
if
n
is not divisible by
\ell
.
Therefore

(4.5)

H(\bullet;x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(\bullet)\,x^{n}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}h_{\ell m}(\bullet)\,x^{\ell m}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\bullet)|\,y^{m},

where we set
y\coloneqq x^{\ell}
on the right-hand side.
It therefore suffices to find the generating function of the sequence
(|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\bullet)|)_{m=1}^{\infty}
, from which we will recover
H(\bullet;x)
by replacing
y
with
x^{\ell}
.
In each proof below, we adopt standard interval notation restricted to the integers.

Type
\mathsf{A}
is the most complicated case.
Following Theorem
4.1
, the index set
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{A})
can be constructed in three stages.
First take the ordered pairs
(i,j)
in
[1,m/2)\times[1,m/2)
, of which there are
\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor^{2}
many; remove the diagonal pairs
(i,i)
, of which there are
\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor
many.
Thus after this first stage, using the identities in (
4.4
), the generating function for the number of elements is given by

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor^{2}\,y^{m}-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor y^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{3}(1+y^{2})}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})^{2}}-\frac{y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})}

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{2y^{5}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})^{2}}.

As our second stage in constructing
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{A})
, we remove all pairs
(i,m/3)
, of which there are
\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor-1
many if
m=3p
for some positive integer
p
, and zero otherwise; the generating function for this number is

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\left(\lfloor(3p-1)/2\rfloor-1\right)y^{3p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(p-1)(y^{3})^{p}+\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(p-1)/2\rfloor(y^{3})^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(y^{3})^{2}}{(1-y^{3})^{2}}+\frac{(y^{3})^{3}}{(1-y^{3})(1-(y^{3})^{2})}

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{6}+2y^{9}}{(1-y^{3})(1-y^{6})}.

As the third stage in constructing
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{A})
, add back the pair
(m/6,m/3)
, of which there is 1 if
m=6q
for some positive integer
q
, and zero otherwise.
This number has the generating function

(4.8)

\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}y^{6q}=\frac{y^{6}}{1-y^{6}}.

Combining these three stages, we have

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{A})|y^{m}=\eqref{A first part}-\eqref{A second part}+\eqref{A third part}=\frac{2y^{5}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})^{2}}-\frac{3y^{9}}{(1-y^{3})(1-y^{6})}.

The result for
H(\mathsf{A};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{2}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{B})=[1,5m/2]\setminus\{m/2,m,3m/2,2m\}
, we have

|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{B})|=\lfloor 5m/2\rfloor-2-2[\text{$m$ is even}]
, where the final bracket is the Iverson bracket (1 if its argument is true, and 0 otherwise).
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{B})|\,y^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(\lfloor 5m/2\rfloor-2)y^{m}-2\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}y^{2p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}2my^{m}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor m/2\rfloor y^{m}-2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}y^{m}-2\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(y^{2})^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{2y}{(1-y)^{2}}+\frac{y^{2}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})}-\frac{2y}{1-y}-\frac{2y^{2}}{1-y^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{2}+4y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{B};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{5}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{C})=[1,m/2]
, we have
|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{C})|=\lfloor m/2\rfloor
.
Therefore

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{C})|y^{m}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor m/2\rfloor y^{m}=\frac{y^{2}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{C};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{6}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{D})=[1,3m/2)\setminus\{m/2,m\}
, we have

|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{D})|=\lfloor(3m-1)/2\rfloor-1-[\text{$m$ is even}]
.
Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{D})|y^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(\lfloor(3m-1)/2\rfloor-1)y^{m}-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}y^{2p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(m-1)\,y^{m}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor y^{m}-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(y^{2})^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{2}}{(1-y)^{2}}+\frac{y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})}-\frac{y^{2}}{1-y^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{D};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{6}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{E})=[1,m/2)
, we have
|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{E})|=\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor
.
Thus

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{E})|y^{m}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor=\frac{y^{3}}{(1-y)(1-y^{2})},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{E};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{6}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{F})=[1,4m)\setminus\{m,2m,3m\}
, we have
|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{F})|=4m-4=4(m-1)
.
Thus

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{F})|y^{m}=4\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(m-1)y^{m}=\frac{4y^{2}}{(1-y)^{2}},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{F};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{8}
.

Since
\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{G})=[1,4]
, we have
|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{G})|=4
for all
m
.
Thus

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|\rsfscr{I}_{m}(\mathsf{G})|y^{m}=4\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}y^{m}=\frac{4y}{1-y},

and the result for
H(\mathsf{G};x)
follows from (
4.5
), upon setting
y=x^{\ell}=x^{20}
.
∎

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
h_{n}
denote the number of nontrivial homometry classes of binary bracelets with length
n
and weight 5.
The generating function
H(x)
of the sequence
(h_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
has the closed form

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}\,x^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2x^{10}}{(1-x^{2})(1-x^{4})^{2}}+\frac{x^{10}+4x^{15}}{(1-x^{5})(1-x^{10})}+\frac{x^{12}+x^{18}}{(1-x^{6})(1-x^{12})}+\frac{4x^{16}}{(1-x^{8})^{2}}+\frac{4x^{20}}{1-x^{20}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.1
, Types
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
are exhaustive and pairwise disjoint.
Therefore we simply add the seven type-specific generating functions to obtain
H(x)=H(\mathsf{A};x)+\cdots+H(\mathsf{G};x)
.
The result follows from Lemma
4.3
, upon some light simplification.
∎

Inspecting the numerators and denominators in the rational functions in Theorem
4.4
, we see that

h_{n}>0\text{ if and only if $n\geq 10$ and $n$ is divisible by $2$ or $5$}.

Concretely, starting at
n=10
, the generating function
H(x)
yields the following initial values of
h_{n}
:

(h_{n})_{n=10}^{\infty}=(3,0,3,0,6,5,10,0,14,0,22,0,20,0,31,10,30,0,30,0,57,0,54,0,56,15,61,0,72,0,108,\ldots).

Note that by replacing the term
x^{18}
with
tx^{18}
in Theorem
4.4
, we can refine
H(x)
with respect to the size of the homometry classes.
That is, the resulting bivariate power series reveals the number
h_{n}^{(2)}
of homometric pairs and the number
h_{n}^{(3)}
of homometric triples, since its expansion is

(4.9)

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(h_{n}^{(2)}+h_{n}^{(3)}t\right)x^{n}.

This follows from the fact that Type
\mathsf{E}
is the only type consisting of homometric triples rather than pairs;
indeed, changing
x^{18}
to
tx^{18}
is the same as changing
H(\mathsf{E};x)
to
t\cdot H(\mathsf{E};x)
in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Remark 4.5
.

The sum of rational functions in Theorem
4.4
is especially amenable to extracting asymptotic information about the values
h_{n}
.
In particular, the five rational functions have poles at
x=1
of orders 3, 2, 2, 2, and 1, respectively;
therefore the pole of order 3 (coming from the first rational function) dominates, and consequently
h_{n}=O(n^{3-1})=O(n^{2})
.
To pin down the constant, we again look at the first rational function in Theorem
4.4
,
and take the limit of its product with
(1-x)^{3}
as
x\rightarrow 1
:

\lim_{x\rightarrow 1}\left[(1-x)^{3}\cdot\frac{2x^{10}}{(1-x^{2})(1-x^{4})^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{16}.

Since the first rational function is a function of
x^{2}
, it contributes nothing to
h_{n}
for
n
odd;
hence we have the asymptotic

(4.10)

h_{n}\sim\frac{n^{2}}{16}\text{ as $n\rightarrow\infty$ (for $n$ even)},

while
h_{n}=O(n)
for
n
odd.
On the left-hand side below, to illustrate (
4.10
), we plot the values of
h_{n}
for
n=1,\ldots,200
, along with the red parabola
n^{2}/16
.
On the right-hand side below, we do the same thing for
n=20,40,60,\ldots,1000
.

5.
Open problems

The results in this paper suggest several avenues for further research.

Problem 5.1
.

Give a combinatorial characterization of the 22 difference tables in
\bigcup_{(p,q)}\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)
.

Essentially, Problem
5.1
aims to obviate the need for Algorithm
3.1
, by finding an
a priori
condition that cuts out a minimal set
\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)
.
As a first step, one can easily write down certain simple sufficient conditions for
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
to be empty; for example, due to the relations (
2.16
), for
h\leq i<j\leq k
, we have
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)=\varnothing
if the entry
\boxed{\mathsf{hk}}
lies weakly southwest of the entry
\boxed{\mathsf{ij}}
.
One can write down similar conditions corresponding to the various inequalities in (
2.17
)–(
2.22
), but there are certainly other less obvious configurations that produce an empty solution set.
Ultimately, if
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
arise from the permutations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, respectively, then one might hope to find a certain condition on
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
that is equivalent to
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)\subseteq\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta^{\prime})
.
Recall that the minimal set of 22 tables which we give in this paper is unique up to substituting tables with identical solution sets; hence it is conceivable that a different choice of representative tables could reveal a combinatorial pattern that is currently hidden.

Problem 5.2
.

Extend the methods in this paper to solve Problem
1.1
for weights
k\geq 6
.

On one hand, theoretically, our methods depended very little on the fact that
k=5
.
On the other hand, the jump from
k=5
to
k=6
presents several practical complications.
First, for
k=6
the number of pairwise distances is
\binom{6}{2}=15
, meaning that the number of permutations of
D
is now
15!\approx 1.3\times 10^{12}
, so that an analogue of Algorithm
3.1
is far less feasible.
(Thus, any hope of solving the
k=6
case seems to depend on first solving Problem
5.1
above.)
Moreover, for
k=6
we would expect (possibly many) more than just five valid pairs
(p,q)
, given the appropriate analogue of “long count” for a cyclic hexagon.
Add to this the fact, mentioned in the introduction, that unlike the
k\leq 5
case, for
k\geq 6
there are homometric subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
with cardinality
k
; since all of these descend to homometric bracelets as well, we would expect this fact to complicate the attempt at solving Problem
1.1
.

Our original draft posed the following problem, which has since been solved by P. Methawisal
[
18
]
:
Obtain a group-theoretic version of the classification in Theorem
4.1
.

Naturally we refer the reader to
[
18
]
for details, but to motivate this problem briefly, we point out the following “hidden symmetries” among homometric bracelets.
Let
U_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\times}
denote the group of multiplicative units in the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and let

\widetilde{U}_{n}\coloneqq U_{n}/\{\pm 1\}.

Then
\widetilde{U}_{n}
acts on a bracelet of length
n
by multiplication (modulo
n
) on any of the bracelet representatives:

u\cdot[s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}]=[us_{1},\ldots,us_{k}].

One can easily show that this gives a well-defined group action of
\widetilde{U}_{n}
on the set of all length-
n
binary bracelets with some fixed weight
k
.
(This is of course true of
U_{n}
as well, but since bracelets are unchanged under reflection, one may as well work modulo sign to obtain a faithful action.)
Moreover, this action preserves the homometry relation, and so it actually extends to a
\widetilde{U}_{n}
-action on the set of all homometry classes of such bracelets.
Relating to this paper, then, a natural problem was to describe the structure of the
\widetilde{U}_{n}
-orbits in the set of nontrivial homometry classes, including a characterization of the fixed points (i.e., nontrivial homometry classes whose bracelets are maximally “symmetrical” with respect to the
\widetilde{U}_{n}
-action).
Moreover, we conjectured the following refinement, which now has been proved in the same paper
[
18
]
cited above:

Theorem 5.3

(Methawisal
[
18
]
*Thm. 3.1)
.

With respect to the action given above, the group
\widetilde{U}_{n}
acts on the set of nontrivial homometry classes of each Type
\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{G}
in Theorem
4.1
.

We again refer the reader to
[
18
]
for a detailed analysis of the
\widetilde{U}_{n}
-action on each of our seven types.

Appendix A
Full example: the difference table
\mathsf{D_{4}}
and its solution set

In this example, we illustrate Definitions
2.7
and
2.8
by constructing a difference table from a permutation
\pi
, and then explicitly writing down the system defining its solution set.
(It turns out that this difference table is the element in
\rsfscr{M}\!(1,2)
that we labeled as
\mathsf{D_{4}}
.)
The example is meant to make Algorithm
3.1
completely transparent and easy to program.

First we follow Definition
2.7
, where
(p,q)=(1,2)
.
Let
\pi
be the permutation of
D
that acts on (
2.15
) as shown in the first step below:

(A.1)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\mathsf\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\mathsf{13}$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&{01}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\mathsf{12}$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 1380.4pt}}$}

Since
p=1
, there is one
p
-long element of
D
, namely
\boxed{\mathsf{04}}
, by (
2.14
).
Thus in the second step in (
A.1
), we place a bar over the entry
\boxed{\mathsf{04}}
in the permuted table.
Finally, since
q=2
, there are two
q
-long elements of
D
, namely
\boxed{\mathsf{03}}
and
\boxed{\mathsf{04}}
, by (
2.14
).
Referring to the original (unpermuted) table on the far left of (
A.1
), we observe the positions of these two entries, namely the two rightmost boxes in the top row.
Thus in the third step in (
A.1
), we place bars over the entries in the corresponding positions in the permuted table, namely
\boxed{\mathsf{14}}
and
\boxed{\mathsf{24}}
.
(If one of these positions had already had a bar, then we would have removed the bar, since two bars cancel each other out.)

The rightmost table in (
A.1
) is the element
\Delta\in\rsfscr{D}\hskip-0.5pt(1,2)
corresponding to the permutation
\pi
.
We should view this
(1,2)
-difference table
\Delta
as a generic pair of homometric 5-point bracelets
[X]
and
[Y]
with long counts
1
and
2
, respectively:

\mathsf{01}
\mathsf{12}
\mathsf{23}
\mathsf{34}
\mathsf{\overline{04}}
\mathsf{02}
\mathsf{03}
\mathsf{13}
\mathsf{14}
\mathsf{24}
[X]
\mathsf{02}
\mathsf{23}
\mathsf{13}
\mathsf{12}
\mathsf{24}
\mathsf{03}
\mathsf{14}
\mathsf{34}
\mathsf{\overline{04}}
\mathsf{01}
[Y]

In the diagrams above, we label each chord with the
distance
(not the difference!) between its endpoints.
Note that these labels are precisely the entries obtained after the
second
step in (
A.1
), since that step renders all entries short.

Next we will construct the solution set
\mathbf{XY}\!_{1,2}(\Delta)
, following Definition
2.8
.
Expanding the shorthand in (
2.17
)–(
2.19
), respectively, where
p=1
, we obtain the following three constraints:

(A.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&0<x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{4},\\
&x_{4}>1/2\text{ and }x_{3}\leq 1/2\text{ and }x_{4}-x_{1}\leq 1/2,\\
&x_{1}\leq x_{4}-x_{3},\text{ and if }x_{1}=x_{4}-x_{3}\text{ then }x_{2}-x_{1}\leq x_{3}-x_{2}.\end{split}

To write out the six equations (
2.20
), we simply refer to the table
\Delta
on the far right of (
A.1
), and force each off-diagonal entry to be the sum of the diagonal entries lying weakly southwest of it:

(A.3)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{aligned} \boxed{\mathsf{03}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{02}}+\boxed{\mathsf{23}},\\
\boxed{\mathsf{34}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{23}}+\boxed{\mathsf{13}},\\
\boxed{\mathsf{01}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{13}}+\boxed{\mathsf{12}},\\
\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{14}}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{02}}+\boxed{\mathsf{23}}+\boxed{\mathsf{13}},\\
\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{04}}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{23}}+\boxed{\mathsf{13}}+\boxed{\mathsf{12}},\\
\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{24}}}&=\boxed{\mathsf{02}}+\boxed{\mathsf{23}}+\boxed{\mathsf{13}}+\boxed{\mathsf{12}}.\end{aligned}&\leadsto&\begin{bmatrix}\phantom{-}0&\phantom{-}0&0&\phantom{-}0\\
-1&-1&2&-1\\
-3&\phantom{-}1&1&\phantom{-}0\\
-2&\phantom{-}0&2&\phantom{-}1\\
-2&\phantom{-}0&2&\phantom{-}1\\
-2&\phantom{-}0&2&\phantom{-}1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
x_{3}\\
x_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
0\\
1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}.\end{array}

To write out (
2.21
), we do the same as we did for (
A.2
) above, where
q=2
and where we take the entries from the positions in
\Delta
corresponding to the original entries in the unpermuted table (
2.15
):

(A.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&1-(x_{4}-x_{1})>1/2\text{ and }x_{3}<1/2\text{ and }1-x_{4}\leq 1/2,\\
&x_{2}\leq 1-(1-(x_{4}-x_{2})),\text{ and if }x_{2}=1-(1-(x_{4}-x_{2}))\text{ then }x_{3}-x_{2}\leq x_{2}-x_{1}.\end{split}

Finally, recalling that
\mathbf{y}(\Delta)
is given by the top row of
\Delta
, namely

(A.5)

\mathbf{y}(\Delta)=\left(\boxed{\mathsf{02}},\boxed{\mathsf{03}},\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{14}}},\boxed{\mathsf{\overline{24}}}\right)=\big(x_{2},\;x_{3},\;1-(x_{4}-x_{1}),\;1-(x_{4}-x_{2})\big),

the constraint (
2.22
) becomes

(A.6)

x_{1}\neq x_{2}\text{ or }x_{2}\neq x_{3}\text{ or }x_{3}\neq 1-(x_{4}-x_{1})\text{ or }x_{4}\neq 1-(x_{4}-x_{2}).

Using software to solve (
A.2
)–(
A.6
) for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
, we obtain the following line segment in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
:

\mathbf{X}_{1,2}(\Delta)=\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
2x_{1}-1/6\\
1/6+x_{1}\\
2/3\end{smallmatrix}\right]:1/6<x_{1}\leq 1/4\right\}.

Thus by (
A.5
), we obtain the solution set

\mathbf{XY}\!_{1,2}(\Delta)=\left\{\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
2x_{1}-1/6\\
1/6+x_{1}\\
2/3\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{\textstyle,}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2x_{1}-1/6\\
1/6+x_{1}\\
1/3+x_{1}\\
1/6+2x_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right):1/6<x_{1}\leq 1/4\right\}.

This concludes the example.
(Compare with the
\Delta=\mathsf{D_{4}}
entry in the
\rsfscr{M}\!(1,2)
table in Appendix
B
.)

Appendix B
The solution sets
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
before reparametrization

In each table below (corresponding to each of the five
(p,q)
pairs in Lemma
2.4
), we write down the explicit solution set
\mathbf{XY}\!_{pq}(\Delta)
for each
\Delta\in\rsfscr{M}\!(p,q)
.
These results are easily obtained by programming the system (
2.17
)–(
2.22
) given in Definition
2.8
.
Each of the 22 solution sets below appears in the proof of Proposition
3.2
, albeit in a carefully reparametrized form.
We provide the “raw data” here so that one can easily verify the reparametrizations given in that proof above.
